Answer briefly:
In the research_team agent, what is the difference between the search_node and the web_scraper_node?
Read c:\Users\LOQ\Desktop\Projects\Bhai-To-Bhai\yt-tutorial\teams\research.py

18 lines of output
Structurally they're identical — both invoke a create_agent agent on the shared State, wrap its report in a HumanMessage, and goto="supervisor" (research.py:35-65). The only differences:

search_node	web_scraper_node
Agent	search_agent	web_scraper_agent
Tool	tavily_tool — Tavily web search, takes a query, returns up to TAVILY_MAX_RESULTS snippets (tools/web.py:12)	scrape_webpages — takes a list of URLs, loads full page text via UnstructuredLoader (tools/web.py:20)
Prompt	SEARCH_AGENT	WEB_SCRAPER_AGENT (prompts.py:50-54)
Message name	"search"	"web_scraper"
So: search discovers sources (query → snippets + URLs), scraper reads them (URLs → full text). The supervisor typically routes search first, then the scraper on the URLs it found.

Read c:\Users\LOQ\Desktop\Projects\Bhai-To-Bhai\yt-tutorial\teams\supervisor.py
Read c:\Users\LOQ\Desktop\Projects\Bhai-To-Bhai\yt-tutorial\main.py
Read c:\Users\LOQ\Desktop\Projects\Bhai-To-Bhai\yt-tutorial\teams\writing.py
Read c:\Users\LOQ\Desktop\Projects\Bhai-To-Bhai\yt-tutorial\teams\orchestrator.py
Three levels, and the key thing is that only 5 things in this project are actual agents — everything else is a plain node or a single LLM call.


main.run()  →  super_graph  [teams/orchestrator.py]
   supervisor  (teams_supervisor_node — router, NOT an agent)
   ├── research_team  (plain node → invokes research_graph)
   │      supervisor  (research_supervisor_node — router)
   │      ├── search          → search_agent        [tavily_tool]
   │      └── web_scraper     → web_scraper_agent   [scrape_webpages]
   └── writing_team   (plain node → invokes writing_graph)
          supervisor  (writing_supervisor_node — router)
          ├── doc_writer      → doc_writer_agent      [write/edit/read_document]
          ├── note_taker      → note_taking_agent     [create_outline, read_document]
          └── chart_generator → chart_generating_agent[read_document, python_repl_tool]
Orchestrator: super_graph (orchestrator.py:66-76) — the only graph main.py runs.

Supervisors are routers, not agents. All three come from the same factory make_supervisor_node (supervisor.py:53). Each is one structured-output LLM call returning {"next": <member|FINISH>} (supervisor.py:73-80). No tools, no loop, no ReAct — it just emits a Command(goto=...). The looping is the graph edge (workers always goto="supervisor"), not the supervisor itself.

Sub-agents (the real agents): the 5 create_agent(...) objects — each has its own model, system prompt, tool set, and tool_call_limit() middleware. These are the only things that run a tool-calling loop.

Just nodes, no LLM at all:

call_research_team / call_writing_team (orchestrator.py:36,52) — pure Python: invoke the compiled sub-graph with its own TEAM_CONFIG recursion budget, take the last message, hand it back to the supervisor.
search_node, web_scraper_node, doc_writing_node, note_taking_node, chart_generating_node — thin wrappers: agent.invoke(state) → agent_report(result) → HumanMessage(name=...) → goto="supervisor".
Isolation detail worth noting: teams don't share the full conversation. Each team node passes only state["messages"][-1] down, and only result["messages"][-1] comes back up (orchestrator.py:38-47). Within a team, workers do see the whole team-level message list.

A2	Content shrank across writes (280 → 374 → 325 chars) — the agent was rewriting, not appending. edit_document was never used.	lines 12-14
A3	Wrote MemPalace Essay.docx — write_document emits plain text, so the .docx is a corrupt file. Extension was invented by the model.	line 12
A4	The 4th tool call was killed by TOOL_CALL_RUN_LIMIT=3, so the report became the agent_report fallback stub ("Finished. Tools executed: …") instead of a real summary.	line 15
A5	Supervisor FINISHed after one worker with zero verification — never read the file back, never checked it matched the request.	line 23
Runs B & C — 20260805_163729 and 20260806_115422 (full pipeline, identical failure)

#	Issue	Evidence
B1	Nothing was ever written. Run finished | steps=5 | documents=none. writing_team was never invoked once.	B:105, C:105
B2	The research supervisor routed to search twice in a row, then a third time — killed only by the max_worker_reports=2 structural cap. Every FINISH in these runs is a forced FINISH (WARNING), never a decision.	B:38,48,80,90
B3	The 2nd and 3rd search calls returned the same content as the 1st. No query diversification — pure wasted budget.	B:40 vs 72 vs 82
B4	web_scraper was never routed to, in any run. Partly because search never emits URLs, so there'd be nothing to scrape anyway.	all
B5	The search agent drops all source URLs. Its report is bare prose, which starves web_scraper and makes grounding unverifiable.	B:51, C:51
B6	Hallucination from parametric memory: reports "Ginger" and "ProWritingAid" as open source. Both are proprietary commercial products. The model completed a plausible 3-item list rather than reporting what search returned.	B:51, C:93
B7	The run-killer. The search agent ended its report with "The tools can be compiled in a file named 'research_results.txt'." The top supervisor read that claim-of-intent as a completed deliverable and never routed to writing_team.	B:51 → B:59,101
B8	Top supervisor routed to research_team a second time instead of writing_team, then hit its own cap and FINISHed.	B:59, B:101
B9	On that second call, call_research_team passes state["messages"][-1] — which is now research_team's own previous report, not the user request. The team re-researched its own output. (Orchestration bug, orchestrator.py:38; not fixable in prompts.)	B:63-66, input drops to 1363 chars
B10	Only result["messages"][-1] propagates up from a team, so everything found in earlier turns is discarded. Combined with A4, a forced FINISH can propagate a bookkeeping stub.	orchestrator.py:44
B11	The required filename research_results.txt never reaches the writing team — it lives only in the original request, which the team never sees.	B9 + B10
B12	No worker ever states what is still missing, so the supervisor has no signal to route on other than "sounds complete".	all
Your diagnosis was right, and B7 is the precise mechanism: the search agent claimed the file work as good-as-done, which is exactly what made the supervisor stop.

2. Prompting techniques extracted from your RAG prompts
Single-job role statement — "Your ONLY job is to produce search-query variants", plus what other stages do, so the model doesn't reach into them.
Stacked negative roles — parallel short "You are NOT a general knowledge assistant / NOT filling gaps from memory / NOT completing a plausible-sounding list because the topic invites one."
Anti-code guards — "You are NOT writing software. You are NOT generating Python." Added because the small model kept emitting code.
Internal classification before output — FULL/PARTIAL/INSUFFICIENT, explicitly marked "internal reasoning, not output".
Admission criteria as a gate — "A claim may appear only if: stated in CONTEXT / not an inference / not a 'typical' fact you know."
Calibration rule — "Length must track evidence": thin context → short answer, and padding is named as the failure.
Paired GOOD/BAD with a Reason: line — same input, right and wrong output, and why. The most heavily used device in the file.
Numbered named rules (RULE 1 … RULE 4) so verdicts and later text can reference them.
PROCESS (follow in this exact order) — ordered steps with GOOD/BAD embedded per step.
Closed verdict vocabulary with precedence — GROUNDED / PARTIALLY_FABRICATED / OVERCLAIMED / OFF_TOPIC / UNKNOWN, each with a precise trigger and a stated tie-break.
Input blocks labeled with their authority — "CONTEXT (the only allowed source of facts)", "DRAFT (working material — not the source of truth)".
Near-miss disambiguation — "Same topic is NOT enough. Related ideas are NOT enough. Similar wording is NOT enough."
Stated error preference — "If unsure, DO NOT group. It is BETTER to miss a redundancy than to create a false match."
Fluency ≠ correctness — "Do not default to GROUNDED because the answer sounds fluent."
Explicit BAD OUTPUTS: list — enumerates observed production failure modes.
Hard output contract with consequences — exact shape, "first character must be '['", "parsed with json.loads()", "invalid JSON will cause failure".
Defaults for missing data — "", 0, [], first enum value — so the model never improvises a placeholder.
Explicit gap-disclosure requirement — say what's missing rather than glossing.
Disagreement handling — "If sources disagree, report the disagreement — do not silently pick one."
Constraint restated at the close — final line repeats the core rule.
System-enforced framing — "HARD LIMITS (enforced by the system — do not test them)."

Issue → fix mapping
Issue	Fix in prompts.py
B7 search agent claiming the file work is done — the run-killer	SEARCH_AGENT / WEB_SCRAPER_AGENT: "Never state or imply that the information has been compiled, saved or written to a file… claiming it makes the run stop with nothing." The BAD example is the verbatim failing sentence.
B8/B1 top supervisor never routing to writing_team	TOP_LEVEL_SCOPE HARD RULE: writing_team MUST run before FINISH if the request names a file; "a research report that MENTIONS the file name is not a file". Plus GOOD/BAD on exactly this transition.
A5/B12 FINISH on a confident-sounding report	SUPERVISOR_BASE EVIDENCE RULE ("CAN be done is not WAS done") + SUPERVISOR_TERMINATION_RULES ("fluent, confident reports are not proof").
B2 repeated routing to the same worker	SUPERVISOR_BASE REPETITION RULE: re-route only if you can name the missing thing.
B3 identical repeat searches	SEARCH_AGENT: one retry max, must use different vocabulary, "not a reworded copy".
B5/B4 no URLs, so web_scraper is unusable	SEARCH_AGENT report format requires [<url>] per item + a Sources: line; RESEARCH_TEAM_SCOPE tells the supervisor web_scraper needs URLs already present.
B6 hallucinated "open source" tools	SEARCH_AGENT admission criteria + "check the qualifier, not just the name" + the Ginger/ProWritingAid BAD example.
A1/A2 four overwriting write_document calls	DOC_WRITER_AGENT: "write_document REPLACES THE WHOLE FILE… a second call DESTROYS everything the first wrote", edit_document for additions, with the 3-call BAD example.
A3 invented .docx	DOC_WRITER_AGENT FILE NAME block: exact name, "write_document writes plain text, so a .docx name produces a broken file".
B11 deliverable filename lost	NOTE_TAKER_AGENT must never write to the deliverable name; DOC_WRITER_AGENT must use the exact requested name.
B12 no "what's missing" signal	SEARCH_AGENT Still missing: line; scraper's failed-pages line.
chart fabrication risk	CHART_GENERATOR_AGENT: "No numeric data available to chart" is a valid result.
Left unfixed — these are code bugs, not prompt bugs
B9 — orchestrator.py:38 passes state["messages"][-1], so a second research_team call re-researches the team's own previous output instead of the user request. No prompt can recover the lost request.
B10 — only result["messages"][-1] propagates up from a team (orchestrator.py:44); earlier findings are discarded.
A4 — when TOOL_CALL_RUN_LIMIT ends a run, agent_report falls back to a tool-name stub. The new report formats reduce how often that path is hit, but don't remove it.
One cost to flag: the top-level supervisor system prompt is now ~3.1k chars (~780 tokens) and is prepended to every routing call — roughly 3× the previous per-turn input. On an 8B model that's a favourable trade for the routing accuracy, but if latency matters you'd trim the GOOD/BAD blocks from SUPERVISOR_BASE first and keep them only in the scopes.

Bash Unit-test agent_report and team_report

import ast, textwrap
from langchain_core.messages import AIMessage, HumanMessage, ToolMessage
from teams.supervisor import agent_report

# --- A4: limit stub last, real answer further back ---
msgs = [
    HumanMessage(content='write the file'),
    AIMessage(content='', tool_calls=[{'name':'write_document','args':{'file_name':'research_results.txt','content':'x'},'id':'1'}]),
    ToolMessage(content='Document saved to: research_results.txt', tool_call_id='1'),
    AIMessage(content='Wrote research_results.txt: 3 tools with descriptions.'),
    AIMessage(content='Tool call limit reached: write_document'),
]
print('A4 ->', repr(agent_report({'messages': msgs})))

# --- no text at all, only tool calls ---
msgs2 = [AIMessage(content='', tool_calls=[{'name':'tavily_search','args':{},'id':'1'}]),
         AIMessage(content='Tool call limit reached: tavily_search')]
print('stub-only ->', repr(agent_report({'messages': msgs2})))

# --- tool result must NOT be mistaken for the worker answer ---
msgs3 = [AIMessage(content='', tool_calls=[{'name':'read_document','args':{'file_name':'a.txt'},'id':'1'}]),
         ToolMessage(content='RAW FILE CONTENTS', tool_call_id='1'),
         AIMessage(content='Tool call limit reached: read_document')]
print('toolmsg-skipped ->', repr(agent_report({'messages': msgs3})))

# --- B10: team_report, exercised from the real source ---
src = open('teams/orchestrator.py', encoding='utf-8').read()
fn = next(n for n in ast.parse(src).body if isinstance(n, ast.FunctionDef) and n.name=='team_report')
ns = {}
exec(compile(ast.Module(body=[fn], type_ignores=[]), 'orchestrator.py', 'exec'), ns)
team_report = ns['team_report']

sent = [HumanMessage(content='original user request incl. research_results.txt')]
result = {'messages': sent + [
    HumanMessage(content='1. LanguageTool -- LGPL [url]', name='search'),
    HumanMessage(content='1. LanguageTool -- LGPL [url]', name='search'),   # duplicate repeat
    HumanMessage(content='Scraped page says X [url]', name='web_scraper'),
]}
print('B10 ->', repr(team_report(result, len(sent))))
print('B10 empty ->', repr(team_report({'messages': sent}, len(sent))))
"

import ast
from langchain_core.messages import AIMessage, HumanMessage, ToolMessage
from teams.supervisor import agent_report

msgs = [
    HumanMessage(content='write the file'),
    AIMessage(content='', tool_calls=[{'name':'write_document','args':{'file_name':'research_results.txt','content':'x'},'id':'1'}]),
    ToolMessage(content='Document saved to: research_results.txt', tool_call_id='1'),
    AIMessage(content='Wrote research_results.txt: 3 tools with descriptions.'),
    AIMessage(content='Tool call limit reached: write_document'),
]
print('A4 ->', repr(agent_report({'messages': msgs})))

msgs2 = [AIMessage(content='', tool_calls=[{'name':'tavily_search','args':{},'id':'1'}]),
         AIMessage(content='Tool call limit reached: tavily_search')]
print('stub-only ->', repr(agent_report({'messages': msgs2})))

msgs3 = [AIMessage(content='', tool_calls=[{'name':'read_document','args':{'file_name':'a.txt'},'id':'1'}]),
         ToolMessage(content='RAW FILE CONTENTS', tool_call_id='1'),
         AIMessage(content='Tool call limit reached: read_document')]
print('toolmsg-skipped ->', repr(agent_report({'messages': msgs3})))

src = open('teams/orchestrator.py', encoding='utf-8').read()
fn = next(n for n in ast.parse(src).body if isinstance(n, ast.FunctionDef) and n.name=='team_report')
ns = {}

c:\Users\LOQ\Desktop\Projects\Bhai-To-Bhai\yt-tutorial\prompts.py
Just plan and dont change:

The search and web_search agents are practically the same. Should there be 2? Is there a way we can keep 2 in the research_team node and make them useful?
Analysis only — no changes made.

Are they the same?
At the tool level, no. At the behavior level in your logs, yes — and the reason matters.

search	web_scraper
Input	a query string	a list of URLs
Output	~5 snippets + URLs	full page text
Can it find pages?	yes	no
Can it read a page in depth?	no	yes
Genuinely complementary. They collapsed into redundancy because of four things, all visible in the logs:

Tavily returns content, not just links. With max_results=5 the snippets are usually enough to write a plausible answer, so search answers instead of discovering. Depth never looks necessary.
search drops the URLs (B5), so web_scraper's only input never enters the transcript. It is structurally unroutable.
Both nodes have identical shape — same wrapper, same agent_report, same prose-summary output. Nothing distinguishes their contribution once it lands in the transcript.
The routing predicate isn't decidable. "Do I need search or scraping?" cannot be answered from prose. The supervisor guessed, and guessed search five times across three runs.
That last one is the general principle: a two-worker team only works if the choice between the workers is a visible property of the transcript. "Are there URLs here yet?" is decidable. "Is this deep enough?" is not.

Should there be two?
Only if the second worker earns its routing hop. There are exactly two things that justify one:

Context isolation. UnstructuredLoader returns entire page bodies — easily 10–50k chars. On an 8B model you do not want that in the same context that's also reasoning about the request. A separate node lets the bulk get summarized down before re-entering the shared transcript. This is a real, non-cosmetic argument for splitting.
A genuinely different job, not a different tool.
If neither applies, one agent with tools=[tavily_tool, scrape_webpages] is strictly better: it keeps the URLs in its own context, needs no handoff, and fits TOOL_CALL_RUN_LIMIT=3 as search → scrape → answer.

Three designs that make two workers real
Option A — Pipeline stages, not alternatives. Redefine the roles so they're sequential.

search becomes a link finder: forbidden to state facts about the topic. Deliverable is a ranked URL list with one-line justifications.
web_scraper becomes the only worker allowed to assert facts, all anchored to page text.
Supervisor rule becomes mechanical: no URLs → search; URLs unscraped → web_scraper; facts present → FINISH.
Fixes B5 and B6 together — search can't hallucinate a tool list because it isn't permitted to write claims.
Stronger variant: replace the supervisor's choice with a hard edge search → web_scraper. Removing the decision removes the failure mode entirely — but then the research supervisor has one entry point and barely earns its own existence.
Cost: two hops minimum, plus UnstructuredLoader latency on every run.
Option B — Merge, then add a genuinely different second worker.

researcher = one agent with both tools (discovery and depth in one context, no lossy handoff).
fact_checker = second agent with tavily_tool, but a completely different job: take the claims already in the transcript, run one targeted search per doubtful claim, report CONFIRMED / REFUTED / UNVERIFIED with the URL.
This attacks the actual failure in your logs. The run didn't fail from shallow content; it failed because "Ginger and ProWritingAid are open source" is false and nothing checked it.
The routing choice becomes decidable and meaningful: are there unverified claims in the transcript?
It also reuses the pattern you already proved works with this model — _RETRIEVAL_JUDGE_PROMPT, _MERGE_JUDGE_PROMPT, GROUNDING_PROMPT are all generate-then-judge with a closed verdict vocabulary. Same technique, new context.
Option C — Collapse to one. research_team becomes a single node with both tools; drop the research supervisor. Cheapest and simplest. Loses context isolation and loses the two-worker team the tutorial is presumably demonstrating.

Recommendation
Option B. It keeps two workers, gives each a job the other cannot do, makes the supervisor's choice decidable from the transcript, and targets the failure you actually observed rather than a hypothetical depth problem. It also stays consistent with the judge-pattern you've already validated against this exact model and server.

If the tutorial specifically needs to demonstrate search-then-scrape, take Option A with the hard edge — but then be honest in the narration that the research supervisor is vestigial there.

One thing worth measuring before committing to either: how large a scrape_webpages result actually is on a typical page. If it's routinely >10k chars, context isolation alone justifies keeping the scraper as its own node, and A and B stop being mutually exclusive — you'd end up with search → web_scraper → fact_checker, which is a three-worker pipeline with a fully decidable routing predicate at each step.

"""Tool call limit middleware for agents."""

from __future__ import annotations

from typing import TYPE_CHECKING, Annotated, Any, Literal

from langchain_core.messages import AIMessage, ToolCall, ToolMessage
from langgraph.channels.untracked_value import UntrackedValue
from langgraph.typing import ContextT
from typing_extensions import NotRequired, override

from langchain.agents.middleware.types import (
    AgentMiddleware,
    AgentState,
    PrivateStateAttr,
    ResponseT,
    hook_config,
)

if TYPE_CHECKING:
    from langgraph.runtime import Runtime

ExitBehavior = Literal["continue", "error", "end"]
"""How to handle execution when tool call limits are exceeded.

- `'continue'`: Block exceeded tools with error messages, let other tools continue
    (default)
- `'error'`: Raise a `ToolCallLimitExceededError` exception
- `'end'`: Stop execution immediately, injecting a `ToolMessage` and an `AIMessage` for
    the single tool call that exceeded the limit. Raises `NotImplementedError` if there
    are other pending tool calls (due to parallel tool calling).
"""


class ToolCallLimitState(AgentState[ResponseT]):
    """State schema for `ToolCallLimitMiddleware`.

    Extends `AgentState` with tool call tracking fields.

    The count fields are dictionaries mapping tool names to execution counts. This
    allows multiple middleware instances to track different tools independently. The
    special key `'__all__'` is used for tracking all tool calls globally.

    Type Para
=========== ExitBehavior / limit reached handling ===========
ExitBehavior = Literal["continue", "error", "end"]
"""How to handle execution when tool call limits are exceeded.

- `'continue'`: Block exceeded tools with error messages, let other tools continue
    (default)
- `'error'`: Raise a `ToolCallLimitExceededError` exception
- `'end'`: Stop execution immediately, injecting a `ToolMessage` and an `AIMessage` for
    the single tool call that exceeded the limit. Raises `NotImplementedError` if there
    are other pending tool calls (due to parallel tool calling).
"""


class ToolCallLimitState(AgentState[ResponseT]):
    """State schema for `ToolCallLimitMiddleware`.

    Extends `AgentState` with tool call tracking fields.

    The count fields are dictionaries mapping tool names to execution counts. This
    allows multiple middleware instances to track different tools independently. The
    special key `'__all__'` is used for tracking all t

OUT
    temp_thread_count += 1
                temp_run_count += 1

        return allowed_calls, blocked_calls, temp_thread_count, temp_run_count

    @hook_config(can_jump_to=["end"])
    @override
    def after_model(
        self,
        state: ToolCallLimitState[ResponseT],
        runtime: Runtime[ContextT],
    ) -> dict[str, Any] | None:
        """Increment tool call counts after a model call and check limits.

        Args:
            state: The current agent state.
            runtime: The langgraph runtime.

        Returns:
            State updates with incremented tool call counts. If limits are exceeded
                and exit_behavior is `'end'`, also includes a jump to end with a
                `ToolMessage` and AI message for the single exceeded tool call.

        Raises:
            ToolCallLimitExceededError: If limits are exceeded and `exit_behavior`
                is `'error'`.
            NotImplementedError: If limits are exceeded, `exit_behavior` is `'end'`,
                and there are multiple tool calls.
        """
        # Get the last AIMessage to check for tool calls
        messages = state.get("messages", [])
        if not messages:
            return None

        # Find the last AIMessage
        last_ai_message = None
        for message in reversed(messages):
            if isinstance(message, AIMessage):
                last_ai_message = message
                break

        if not last_ai_message or not last_ai_message.tool_calls:
            return None

        # Get the count key for this middleware instance
        count_key = self.tool_name or "__all__"

        # Get current counts
        thread_counts = state.get("thread_tool_call_count", {}).copy()
        run_counts = state.get("run_tool_call_count", {}).copy()
        current_thread_count = thread_counts.get(count_key, 0)
        current_run_count = run_counts.get(count_key, 0)

        # Separate tool calls into allowed and blocked
        allowed_calls, blocked_calls, new_thread_count, new_run_count = self._separate_tool_calls(
            last_ai_message.tool_calls, current_thread_count, current_run_count
        )

        # Update counts to include only allowed calls for thread count
        # (blocked calls don't count towards thread-level tracking)
        # But run count includes blocked calls since they were attempted in this run
        thread_counts[count_key] = new_thread_count
        run_counts[count_key] = new_run_count + len(blocked_calls)

        # If no tool calls are blocked, just update counts
        if not blocked_calls:
            if allowed_calls:
                return {
                    "thread_tool_call_count": thread_counts,
                    "run_tool_call_count": run_counts,
                }
            return None

        # Get final counts for building messages
        final_thread_count = thread_counts[count_key]
        final_run_count = run_counts[count_key]

        # Handle different exit behaviors
        if self.exit_behavior == "error":
            # Use hypothetical thread count to show which limit was exceeded
            hypothetical_thread_count = final_thread_count + len(blocked_calls)
            raise ToolCallLimitExceededError(
                thread_count=hypothetical_thread_count,
                run_count=final_run_count,
                thread_limit=self.thread_limit,
                run_limit=self.run_limit,
                tool_name=self.tool_name,
            )

        # Build tool message content (sent to model - no thread/run details)
        tool_msg_content = _build_tool_message_content(self.tool_name)

        # Inject artificial error ToolMessages for blocked tool calls
        artificial_messages: list[ToolMessage | AIMessage] = [
            ToolMessage(
                content=tool_msg_content,
                tool_call_id=tool_call["id"],
                name=tool_call.get("name"),
                status="error",
            )
            for tool_call in blocked_calls
        ]

        if self.exit_behavior == "end":
            # Check if there are tool calls to other tools that would continue executing
            other_tools = [
                tc
                for tc in last_ai_message.tool_calls
                if self.tool_name is not None and tc["name"] != self.tool_name
            ]

            if other_tools:

        return f"Tool call limit exceeded. Do not call '{tool_name}' again."
    return "Tool call limit exceeded. Do not make addit

super_graph OK, nodes: ['__end__', '__start__', 'research_team', 'supervisor', 'writing_team']
Fixed. The chain, and what each fix breaks:

Root cause
search never wrote a report. It spent all 4 turns calling Tavily and exit_behavior="end" cut it off the moment the budget ran out — so the research team's entire output was Finished. Tools executed: tavily_search ×4. The writing team then had exactly one piece of prose in its transcript: the user's request. It wrote that. note_taker, with nothing to outline, outlined its own system prompt.

Two of the three contributing causes were mine, introduced last turn:

My SEARCH_AGENT prompt said "run ONE more search using DIFFERENT vocabulary" — with TOOL_CALL_RUN_LIMIT=3 that guarantees the budget is gone before the report.
My B9 fix correctly passes the full history down, which is what put the request in front of doc_writer as the only available text. Correct change, but it turned an empty-research run from "writes nothing" into "writes the request".
Fixes
1. exit_behavior "end" → "continue" (supervisor.py:25). The middleware now blocks further tool calls with an error ToolMessage ("Tool call limit exceeded. Do not make additional tool calls.") and keeps the agent loop alive, so the worker gets its turn to write up what it found. This is the actual root-cause fix. Blocked calls still count toward the budget, so a model that keeps hammering a blocked tool still runs out.

2. agent_report salvages tool results when there's no prose at all. Verified against a reconstruction of the 12:32 failure:


No written report -- raw tool results follow.

[tavily_search] LanguageTool is open source (LGPL). https://languagetool.org
Previously that identical input produced only the tool-name stub. The artificial limit-exceeded ToolMessage is filtered out (status == "error"), and duplicate results collapse. 17 seconds of real search results no longer get discarded.

3. single_call_limit(tool_name) — new, run_limit=1 on write_document for doc_writer and create_outline for note_taker (writing.py). doc_writer wrote 759 → 859 → 205 chars and the last one won; the clobbering is now impossible rather than merely discouraged, and the first (fullest) write survives. read_document/edit_document are unaffected, so the read-then-edit path still works.

4. Prompts — removed the budget-exhausting "run ONE more search"; replaced with an explicit TOOL BUDGET block ("spend AT MOST 2, then STOP and write your report; your report is a plain text message, never a tool call"). Added INSTRUCTIONS AND REQUESTS ARE NOT CONTENT to both writers, each with a defined empty-case output — "No findings were gathered for this request." for doc_writer, and don't call the tool at all for note_taker. That's the pattern from your RAG prompts (__IRRELEVANT__, "No URLs available to scrape."): give the model a legal way to produce nothing, or it invents something.

Residual risk
"continue" means a model that never emits text would loop on blocked calls until the agent's default recursion limit and raise GraphRecursionError — a visible crash rather than a silent stub. I judged this acceptable: the model produced prose in 3 of your 4 runs, blocked calls burn the budget, and fix 2 makes the stub case harmless anyway. If it does happen, the fix is an explicit recursion_limit on the agent.invoke() calls.

The pipeline actually worked this time — research_results.txt has three genuinely open-source tools (LibreOffice Writer, Etherpad, Joplin) with licenses and a source URL. That's the deliverable, and search produced real grounded findings. Two defects remain, both from the same cause.

The crash: after the successful 1459-char write at 12:46:34, the model attempted a second write_document and degenerated into a runaway backslash loop (...\\\\\\\\\\\\...{ }) for two full minutes until TGI's constrained JSON decoder rejected it: 422 invalid escape at column 2715.

The same cause is visible in the file you have open: it contains literal \n — two characters — instead of line breaks. The model writes newlines as escaped sequences in the JSON tool argument, and this server's decoder passes them through raw. The crash is that same escaping going unbounded.

And critically: llm_invoke has a full error taxonomy including UnprocessableEntityError, but create_agent calls model_.invoke() directly inside LangChain's factory — workers bypass all of it, so one provider error kills the whole graph.

import openai, httpx
from langchain_core.messages import AIMessage
from teams.supervisor import run_worker

class Boom:
    def invoke(self, state):
        raise openai.UnprocessableEntityError(
            \"Error code: 422 - {'error': 'Tool error: Failed to parse generated text: invalid escape at line 1 column 2715 \\\"\" + chr(92)*3000 + \"\\\"'}\",
            response=httpx.Response(422, request=httpx.Request('POST','http://x')), body=None)

class Fine:
    def invoke(self, state):
        return {'messages': [AIMessage(content='Wrote research_results.txt: 3 tools.')]}

r = run_worker(Boom(), 'doc_writer', {'messages': []})
print('FAILED WORKER ->', r)
print('report length:', len(r), '(the raw error was ~3000+ chars)')
print()
print('OK WORKER     ->', run_worker(Fine(), 'doc_writer', {'messages': []}))

Fixed, all three graphs build. The good news first: the last run actually delivered. research_results.txt has LibreOffice Writer, Etherpad, and Joplin with licenses and a source URL — real, grounded, open-source tools. exit_behavior="continue" did exactly what it was meant to: search got its turn and wrote a report instead of dying as a stub.

Two defects remained, both from the same root: this server's constrained decoder passes the model's JSON escapes through raw.

1. The crash — workers had no error handling at all
create_agent calls model_.invoke() directly inside LangChain's factory, so llm_invoke's error taxonomy — which already classifies UnprocessableEntityError → LLMErrorKind.UNPROCESSABLE — never sees a worker's turns. One bad generation aborted the entire graph, discarding every team's completed work.

New run_worker(agent, name, state) in supervisor.py; all five worker nodes now go through it. Tested with the actual 422:


Worker 'doc_writer' failed with UnprocessableEntityError and produced no report.
Anything it saved before failing is still on disk. Detail: Error code: 422 - ...
440 chars instead of a 3000-char backslash dump, full traceback still logged. A supervisor can route around a failed worker; it cannot route around a traceback. Each node also dropped 4 lines of duplicated logging.

2. The literal \n in your open file — same cause, visible outcome
The model writes a line break inside a JSON tool argument as the two characters \ and n, and they arrive verbatim. Unbounded, that same habit is the backslash run that crashed the request. _unescape() in tools/documents.py now normalizes at the point of writing, applied to write_document, create_outline, and edit_document. Verified against the real damaged file:


literal-backslash-n before: 3 -> after: 0
real newlines before: 0 -> after: 4
runaway run (40 backslashes) -> 'platforms.\nEtherpad -- real-time editor.'
plain text untouched -> 'C:\Users, 50% done'
A lone backslash before a non-escape letter is left alone, so Windows paths and percentages survive.

3. Stopping the second write before it degenerates
single_call_limit blocks execution but the model still generates the call first — which is where the two-minute degeneration happened. So write_document and create_outline now say it in their return value, where a small model actually notices:

Document saved to: research_results.txt (1459 chars). The document is complete. Do not call write_document again -- report what you wrote.

Tool results are far more salient to an 8B model than system-prompt rules, and the prompt rule alone demonstrably didn't hold.

Ready for another run whenever you want — temp/research_results.txt still holds the mangled version and will be overwritten.

effective per-request timeout: None

ChatOpenAI accepts timeout -> 150.0
Confirmed. The process isn't deadlocked — each worker call can hang for up to 10 minutes.

LLM_RESPONSE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS = 150 is enforced inside llm_invoke, and workers bypass llm_invoke entirely — the same gap that let the 422 kill the run last time. ChatOpenAI was built with no timeout, so every worker call falls back to the OpenAI SDK default of read=600. Your note_taker hung 12:57:08 → 13:03:36 (388s) and was ended by the server's own 500, not by any limit of ours. The httpx client's 5s default never applies — the SDK overrides it per request.

So the worst case is 5 workers × 2 attempts × 600s. It grinds forward; it just does so very slowly.

Two other things in that log worth naming: note_taker wrote its outline to research_results.txt at 12:57:08 — it clobbered the deliverable, exactly what its prompt forbids. And because run_worker now reports the failure honestly, the supervisor's EVIDENCE RULE correctly re-routes to the failed worker, so you pay the hang twice.

The hang — timeout now reaches the client. LLM_RESPONSE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS is passed to both ChatOpenAI and ChatGroq in clients.py, so it applies on both paths to the model — invoker.py for supervisor routing, and the client itself for worker calls that go through create_agent. A stalled generation now fails in 150s instead of 600s, and run_worker turns that into a report the supervisor can route around instead of a stall.


worker/router request timeout: 150.0 s  (was None -> SDK default 600)
The clobbering — create_outline refuses to overwrite a document. Prompt rules didn't hold, so it's structural now. Tested in a sandbox:


outline over the deliverable ->
  Error: research_results.txt already holds a document, not an outline. An outline
  must go in its own file -- call create_outline again with a new name such as outline.txt.
  deliverable intact: 'A real document.'
The error self-corrects (the model picked outline.txt on its own last run anyway), and overwriting an outline with an outline is still allowed, so regeneration works and the guard doesn't break across runs.

You can Ctrl+C the current run — it's mid-hang on a 600s call and everything after this point would be paid at the old timeout.

One thing I'm not fixing yet, because it's a server-side symptom rather than a code bug: the 500 itself. The timeout bounds the damage, and the retry usually succeeds (13:03:38 wrote outline.txt fine). Because at 13:13:13 the file didn't exist yet — so my guard had nothing to protect.

The timeline shows note_taker was routed first, before doc_writer, into a freshly-cleared temp/. My guard only blocks overwriting an existing document; it doesn't stop a worker from claiming the deliverable's name first. research_results.txt now holds note_taker's 4-point outline, and doc_writer never ran.

As for why it picks that name: it's the only filename anywhere in its context. The full-history fix means note_taker sees your request verbatim — "compile in a file named 'research_results.txt'". An explicit, user-authored filename beats a negative rule buried in a system prompt every time on an 8B model. That's the same lesson as the repeated write_document calls: rules the model must remember not to break don't hold; constraints enforced by the tool do.

Fixed with two layers, both verified against the exact case that just failed:

Name guard (new, fires first). create_outline rejects any file name that isn't recognisable as an outline. This is the one that matters here — when note_taker runs before doc_writer into an empty workspace, there is no existing file for the content guard to protect, so it would claim research_results.txt unopposed. Now the deliverable name is never created:


Error: research_results.txt is a deliverable's name, not an outline's. ...
research_results.txt created? False
Content guard (existing, second layer). Still earns its place: it catches a document sitting on an outline-shaped name, which the name check alone would wave through.

Both self-correct — the model reads the error, calls outline.txt, and moves on. That's the behaviour it already showed in the 13:03 run when it retried. Regenerating an existing outline still works, and write_document is untouched, so doc_writer still writes research_results.txt normally.

I also flipped the NOTE_TAKER_AGENT file-name rule from a prohibition to a positive instruction — "Always call create_outline with the file name outline.txt" — and told it the tool rejects anything else. The old negative phrasing ("NEVER write it to the file name the user asked for...") is literally the sentence that got echoed into outline.txt as an outline point back in the 12:33 run, so it was earning its keep in neither direction.

temp/research_results.txt still holds the 4-point outline from the run you're looking at; the next run overwrites it.

# Server that accepts the connection then never replies -- a stalled generation.
srv = socket.socket(); srv.bind(('127.0.0.1', 0)); srv.listen(5)
port = srv.getsockname()[1]
threading.Thread(target=lambda: [srv.accept() for _ in range(3)], daemon=True).start()

c = ChatOpenAI(base_url=f'http://127.0.0.1:{port}/v1', api_key='k', model='m',
               timeout=4, max_retries=0, http_client=build_http_client())
t0 = time.time()
try:
    c.invoke('hi')
except Exception as e:
    print(f'raised after {time.time()-t0:.1f}s -> {type(e).__name__}')
" 2>&1 | tail -3

raised after 4.0s -> APITimeoutError
Timeout propagation is fine — it raises in exactly the configured time even through the TGI transport. So something else is holding it. Let me check whether the process is even still alive and moving.

Found it. The 150s setting is a per-read timeout, not a deadline.

httpx resets that clock on every byte received. This server accepts the request, keeps the socket alive while it generates, and answers whenever it's done — so the read timeout never trips. That's consistent with the earlier failure too: you got a real HTTP 500 after 388 seconds, not a timeout. If the connection had genuinely gone silent for 150s we'd have seen APITimeoutError instead.

My isolated test raised in exactly 4.0s because that server sent nothing at all. Against a server that stays alive while it thinks, the same setting is unbounded. And the source mtimes confirm the running process does have the fix — it just doesn't do what's needed here.

There's a second copy of this bug: _invoke_once wraps its call in with ThreadPoolExecutor(...), and __exit__ calls shutdown(wait=True) — so after LLMResponseTimeoutError is raised it blocks waiting for the very call it's trying to escape. The supervisor path's timeout is decorative for the same reason.

import time, threading
import config
config.WORKER_DEADLINE_SECONDS = 3
import importlib, teams.supervisor as S
importlib.reload(S)
from langchain_core.messages import AIMessage

class Hangs:
    'A server that keeps the socket alive while it generates -- read timeout never fires.'
    def invoke(self, state):
        time.sleep(60); return {'messages':[AIMessage(content='too late')]}

class Fine:
    def invoke(self, state):
        return {'messages':[AIMessage(content='Wrote research_results.txt: 3 tools.')]}

t0 = time.time()
r = S.run_worker(Hangs(), 'note_taker', {'messages': []})
print(f'returned after {time.time()-t0:.1f}s (call still running in background)')

print()
t0 = time.time()

Bash Verify invoker timeout no longer blocks on shutdown
IN
cd "c:/Users/LOQ/Desktop/Projects/Bhai-To-Bhai/yt-tutorial" && .venv/Scripts/python.exe -c "
import time
import llm.invoker as inv
from langchain_core.messages import AIMessage

class Fast:
    max_tokens = 2048
    def invoke(self, messages, config=None, **kw): return AIMessage(content='routed')
class Slow:
    max_tokens = 2048
    def invoke(self, messages, config=None, **kw): time.sleep(60); return AIMessage(content='late')

inv.LLM_RESPONSE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS = 2
r = inv._invoke_once(Fast(), [{'role':'user','content':'hi'}], caller_tag='supervisor')
print('normal path ->', r.ok, repr(r.content))

t0 = time.time()
r = inv._invoke_once(Slow(), [{'role':'user','content':'hi'}], caller_tag='supervisor')
print(f'timeout path -> ok={r.ok} kind={r.error_kind} after {time.time()-t0:.1f}s (was: blocked 60s on shutdown)')
" 2>&1 | grep -v "^  \[supervisor\]\|^\[supervisor\]" | tail -4

OUT
normal path -> True 'routed'
timeout path -> ok=False kind=LLMErrorKind.TIMEOUT after 2.0s (was: blocked 60s on shutdown)
Fixed. LLM_RESPONSE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS is a per-read timeout, not a deadline — httpx restarts that clock on every byte, so a server that holds the socket open while it generates never trips it, no matter how long it thinks. That's why the earlier failure came back as a real HTTP 500 after 388s instead of a timeout, and why this run sat for 9 minutes untouched. The source mtimes confirm the running process did have the setting; it just can't do this job.

WORKER_DEADLINE_SECONDS = 180 — a wall-clock ceiling on a whole worker turn, covering all its model and tool calls together, enforced in run_worker (the single choke point all five workers already go through). Verified against a call that hangs 60s with a 3s deadline:


returned after 3.0s -> Worker 'note_taker' ran past its 3s deadline and produced no
report. Anything it saved before then is still on disk.
normal worker -> Wrote research_results.txt: 3 tools. (0.00s)
The supervisor gets a report it can route around instead of the graph stalling. Normal turns run 10–40s, so 180s only catches the pathological ones.

The same bug in _invoke_once, which is why the supervisor path's timeout was decorative: with ThreadPoolExecutor(...) calls shutdown(wait=True) on exit, blocking on the exact call the timeout exists to escape. Now shutdown(wait=False, cancel_futures=True) in a finally:


timeout path -> ok=False kind=LLMErrorKind.TIMEOUT after 2.0s (was: blocked 60s on shutdown)
The honest tradeoff: an abandoned turn's thread keeps running until the server finally answers — the test shows worker-note_taker_0 still alive after run_worker returned. Nothing else can cancel a blocking socket read in CPython. It costs one idle thread and one server slot until the response lands; waiting for it instead would cost the deadline twice over, which is the bug being fixed.

Also worth noting from that log: the name guard worked — note_taker wrote outline.txt, not research_results.txt. Kill the current run and restart when you're ready; research_results.txt still holds the stale outline from 13:13.

Read the complete log file and find why does it keep getting stuck while writing?

Read the whole log. The pattern is unmistakable, and the rejected generation proves the cause.

Every writing-team turn fails the same way — right after its tool call succeeds:

Worker	Tool call	What followed	Time
doc_writer #1	13:33:27 write_document (1101 chars) ✓	422 UnprocessableEntityError	102s
note_taker	13:35:13 create_outline refused	APITimeoutError	169s
doc_writer #2	13:38:12 write_document (822 chars) ✓	hit the 180s deadline	171s
Meanwhile search completes in 17–20s every time. So it isn't "writing" that's slow — it's what the model generates after a writing tool returns.

The rejected generation:


EOF while parsing a string at line 1 column 3725
{"function": {"_name": "write_document", "content": "Etherpad -- ...
  \\\\[https://opensource.com/life/14/5/seven-open-source-tools-writing\\]
  \\\\\\\\[https://www.getapp.com/all-software/ai-writing-assistant/p/open-source\\]
  \\\\\\\\[https://opensource.com/..., https://www.getapp.com/...\\]
  \\\\\\\\[https://www.getapp.com/all-software/ai-writ     ← cut off mid-URL
Two facts in there. "EOF while parsing a string" means the model never closed the JSON string — it ran out at max_tokens=2048. And the content is the same two URLs repeated over and over, with the backslash escaping doubling on each pass (\\ → \\\\ → \\\\\\\\).

That is a degenerate repetition loop, and it explains all three symptoms at once: 2048 tokens of garbage under grammar-constrained decoding takes 100–180s (the "stuck"), the truncation leaves an unterminated string (the 422), and the same loop with nothing reaching the socket is the read timeout.

Why it loops — two causes, both fixable:

Sampling. temperature=0.1, frequency_penalty=None, presence_penalty=None. Near-greedy decoding with no repetition penalty is the textbook recipe for this on an 8B model.

The input primes it. My SEARCH_AGENT format puts [url] after every item and adds a Sources: line. In this run that made https://www.getapp.com/all-software/ai-writing-assistant/p/open-source appear four times in doc_writer's context. A low-temperature model latches onto exactly that kind of repeated span — and since each repeat adds backslashes, it can never converge back out.

search escapes it because it writes short prose in a plain message, not a long string inside a constrained JSON argument.

Diagnosis complete, with the fix applied.

The answer: it isn't stuck writing — it's stuck in a repetition loop generating the next tool call after a write succeeds. Every writing-team turn in that log fails the same way, always immediately after its tool call returned, always 100–180s later. search never does, because it writes short prose in a plain message rather than a long string inside a grammar-constrained JSON argument.

The rejected generation names the mechanism exactly: EOF while parsing a string at line 1 column 3725 — the model never closed the JSON string because it exhausted max_tokens=2048 re-emitting the same two URLs with the escaping doubling each pass (\\ → \\\\ → \\\\\\\\). One loop produces all three symptoms: the long stall is 2048 tokens of garbage under constrained decoding, the 422 is the truncation, and the APITimeoutError is the same loop with nothing reaching the socket in time.

Two fixes, matching the two causes:

LLM_FREQUENCY_PENALTY = 0.4, now on the wire — confirmed in the payload:


{'model': 'llama-3.1-8b-instruct', 'frequency_penalty': 0.4, 'temperature': 0.1, 'max_completion_tokens': 2048}
Penalising by token count is what actually breaks a repetition loop. I left temperature at 0.1 deliberately — the supervisors need determinism for routing, and changing two sampling knobs at once would make the next run unreadable.

Removed the Sources: line from SEARCH_AGENT's report format. That line is what primed the loop: it made https://www.getapp.com/all-software/ai-writing-assistant/p/open-source appear four times in doc_writer's context, and a repeated span in the input is exactly what a near-greedy model latches onto. Each URL is now cited once, on the item it supports, with a BAD example showing the triple-citation case.

Also restored the request id in worker-thread logs ([-] → [abc12345] verified) — that was a regression from the deadline thread I added, and it matters more now that abandoned turns keep logging in the background.

Worth being straight about two things: I can't verify the penalty end-to-end without your server, and if this TGI build rejects frequency_penalty you'll see an immediate 422 on the first call — it's one line in config.py to back out. And the frequency penalty reduces the probability of the loop rather than making it impossible; the 180s deadline and run_worker's error handling remain the backstop, which is why the run above still finished its writing team instead of hanging forever.

import time, sys
from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor
ex = ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=1)

start=$(date +%s); timeout 12 "c:/Users/LOQ/Desktop/Projects/Bhai-To-Bhai/yt-tutorial/.venv/Scripts/python.exe" exitrepro.py; rc=$?; echo "exit code $rc after $(( $(date +%s) - start ))s (124 = still hanging)"

OUT
run finished -- main() returned
exit code 124 after 12s (124 = still hanging)
Confirmed — reproduced exactly: "run finished" prints, then the process hangs and has to be killed.

shutdown(wait=False) doesn't do what its name suggests. concurrent.futures.thread registers an atexit hook that joins every worker thread, and those threads are non-daemon. So abandoning a turn leaves a thread blocked in its socket read, and the interpreter refuses to exit until it returns. Your two abandoned note_taker turns (13:51:37 and 13:54:39) are still sitting in their LLM calls, holding the process open.

The fix is a daemon thread, which is neither registered with that hook nor joined at exit:

Edit c:\Users\LOQ\Desktop\Projects\Bhai-To-Bhai\yt-tutorial\llm\invoker.py
Added 45 lines
Edit c:\Users\LOQ\Desktop\Projects\Bhai-To-Bhai\yt-tutorial\llm\invoker.py

Removed 1 line
Edit c:\Users\LOQ\Desktop\Projects\Bhai-To-Bhai\yt-tutorial\llm\invoker.py
Removed 3 lines

import time, sys
sys.path.insert(0, r"c:\Users\LOQ\Desktop\Projects\Bhai-To-Bhai\yt-tutorial")
import logging_config as L, teams.supervisor as S
from langchain_core.messages import AIMessage
L.set_request_id("abc12345")
import config; config.WORKER_DEADLINE_SECONDS = 2
import importlib; importlib.reload(S)

class Hangs:
    def invoke(self, state):
        print("  worker thread sees request id:", L.get_request_id(), flush=True)
        time.sleep(120)
class Fine:
    def invoke(self, state): return {"messages": [AIMessage(content="Wrote research_results.txt.")]}
class Boom:
    def invoke(self, state): raise ValueError("provider exploded")

t0 = time.time(); print(S.run_worker(Hangs(), "note_taker", {"messages": []})); print(f"  -> returned in {time.time()-t0:.1f}s")
print(S.run_worker(Fine(), "doc_writer", {"messages": []}))
print(S.run_worker(Boom(), "search", {"messages": []})[:60])
print("main() done -- process should now exit immediately", flush=True)
PY

SP="c:/Users/LOQ/AppData/Local/Temp/claude/c--Users-LOQ-Desktop-Projects-Bhai-To-Bhai/98cbecfb-8b92-479d-97f3-80ee257585a2/scratchpad" && cd "c:/Users/LOQ/Desktop/Projects/Bhai-To-Bhai/yt-tutorial" && start=$(date +%s); timeout 25 .venv/Scripts/python.exe "$SP/dl.py" 2>/dev/null; rc=$?; echo "--- exit $rc after $(( $(date +%s) - start ))s (124 = still hanging; ~9s of that is langchain import) ---"

OUT
  worker thread sees request id: abc12345
DEADLINE: Worker 'note_taker' ran past its 2s deadline and produced no report. Anything it saved before then is still on disk. [2.0s]

--- exit 0 after 10s (124 = still hanging; ~9s of that is langchain import) ---
Fixed. The run wasn't stuck — it finished at 13:54:51. The process exit was stuck, and that was my bug.

shutdown(wait=False, cancel_futures=True) doesn't do what its name implies. concurrent.futures.thread registers an atexit hook that joins every worker thread it ever created, and those threads are non-daemon. So each abandoned turn left a thread blocked in a socket read, and the interpreter refused to exit until it returned. Your two abandoned note_taker turns (13:51:37 and 13:54:39) were still holding it open. Reproduced in isolation:


run finished -- main() returned
exit code 124 after 12s   (124 = still hanging)
The fix: run_with_deadline() in invoker.py uses a daemon thread instead. Daemon threads are neither registered with that hook nor joined at shutdown, so an abandoned call can't outlive the run. Nothing in CPython can cancel a thread blocked on a socket read, so the only real question was whether it survives the process — and now it doesn't.

Both timeout paths share it now (the worker deadline and _invoke_once), which also removed the duplicated thread plumbing. Verified end to end:


worker thread sees request id: abc12345
DEADLINE: Worker 'note_taker' ran past its 2s deadline and produced no report... [2.0s]
NORMAL  : Wrote research_results.txt.
ERROR   : Worker 'search' failed with ValueError and produced no report...
main() done -- process should exit now even though a worker still sleeps
--- exit 0 after 10s ---
Deadline fires on time, normal and error paths unchanged, request id still propagates, and the process exits cleanly with a worker mid-sleep(120). I also introduced a dedicated DeadlineExceededError rather than catching the builtin TimeoutError, so this can never swallow a genuine transport timeout.

Two things that run tells us, separate from the hang:

The frequency penalty didn't rescue note_taker — both its turns still burned the full 180s. Worth noting the first one was after create_outline was refused for the filename, so it was regenerating from an error result, not from a big document. That's a different trigger than the doc_writer loop I diagnosed last time, and it means the repetition penalty alone isn't sufficient.

And documents=['outline.txt'] — no research_results.txt. The writing supervisor picked note_taker first and spent both its turns there, so doc_writer never ran at all. That's the WRITING_TEAM_SCOPE rule ("route doc_writer FIRST when the request just asks for a document") losing to the model's own preference. Say the word and I'll dig into either.

Is it fixed?
Let me check one thing before answering, because it changes the answer.

So what's genuinely fixed is the failure handling: a bad worker turn is now bounded (180s), reported instead of crashing, can't clobber the deliverable, can't corrupt escapes, and can't hold the process open. The pipeline survives its own failures. It just still has them.

The honest gap in my diagnosis: I identified the repetition loop from a 422 body that quoted the rejected text. Verified against the installed binary and the project's own docs. Here's the plan.

Verdict: yes, possible — but it's Stage 6 work, and the project is at Stage 0
maestro delegate on the installed 0.5.61 gives you exactly the primitives needed — --to codex|claude, --role, --mode analysis|write, --async, --resume, plus status/output/cancel/message. I confirmed these against node_modules/.bin/maestro delegate --help, not just the docs, and they match delegate-async-guide.md.

The catch: Build-Guide.md:201 puts the Agent Router at Stage 6, and it routes on ledger state, headroom, and switching cost — all products of Stages 1–5, none of which exist. Building the full thing now means building it on nothing.

What is buildable now is the deterministic tier, which Build-Guide.md:207 says comes first anyway ("Deterministic rules first… an LLM router is a fallback for ambiguous cases only"). That has no dependency on the ledger. So the plan below builds that, with a seam where Stage 5 plugs in.

Five constraints found that shape the design
These aren't three peers — they're two tiers. The local server is an OpenAI-compatible chat endpoint: no repo access, no file writes, no resumable session. Codex/Claude are agentic CLIs with all three. Routing is a capability gate first, a preference table second.

claude.enabled is false in ~/.maestro/cli-tools.json — only codex is enabled. Build-Guide.md:171 already flags flipping this as Stage 4 work.

Never pass --role without --to. Maestro's own resolution order is --to > --role > first enabled tool. With claude disabled, --role plan would silently resolve to codex — maestro overriding your router's decision, invisibly. The router must always emit explicit --to.

Maestro isn't on PATH (ADR-007, repo-local). preflight.py:219 probes bare "maestro", which is one of the two checks currently failing (Bugs.md #3). The router must invoke node_modules/.bin/maestro.

No silent downgrade to local. If a task needs repo writes and both CLIs are unavailable, the router must return unroutable and exit non-zero — not fall back to a model that structurally cannot write files. This is the README's central lesson (the run that exited 0 and produced no document) applied to routing.

The design
Data structures first. All environment facts get gathered into one Capabilities snapshot; the decision itself is a pure function over (RouteRequest, Capabilities) → RouteDecision. No subprocesses inside the decision, so it's unit-testable without a live Mongo, endpoint, or CLI.


RouteRequest:   task_text, role|None, needs_repo_write, needs_filesystem,
                needs_session_resume, explicit_to|None
Capabilities:   per-target {enabled, binary_resolved, reachable, detail}
RouteDecision:  target, tier, reason[], maestro_argv|None, confidence, unroutable_cause|None
Precedence, highest first:

#	Gate	Effect
1	Explicit --to	Honored, but still validated against 2–3
2	Capability	needs_repo_write/filesystem/resume eliminates local
3	Availability	enabled:true + binary resolves (CLIs) / endpoint responds (local)
4	Role table	Picks preferred among survivors
5	Ambiguous	LLM fallback (Phase B) → else configured default
6	No survivor	unroutable + reason, exit 1
Role table, taken from Architecture.md:58 rather than invented:

Role	→	Mode
plan, architect, design, review	claude	analysis
implement, fix, refactor, test	codex	write
classify, summarize, extract, rank	local	—
Files
One module, orchestrator/router.py, mirroring preflight.py's existing shape — stdlib-only, from __future__ import annotations, frozen dataclasses, main() -> int with argparse. Four files would be premature abstraction at this size; the natural split point is when Stage 5's ledger lands.

It reuses _load_cli_tools, _resolve_executable, and _read_json from preflight — which means lifting those three into a shared helper both import, rather than duplicating. That's the one refactor the plan requires, and it's small.

It must not import from yt-tutorial/ — README:26 says the two tracks share no code, and the sandbox is slated for deletion at Stage 1.

CLI:


python orchestrator/router.py --task "..." [--role implement] [--needs-write]
                              [--to codex] [--json] [--explain]
Decides and prints the exact maestro delegate argv. Execution stays opt-in behind a later --execute — keeping the decision observable and testable on its own.

Phasing
Phase A — now. Deterministic router, probe, CLI, unit tests over the pure function. Zero new dependencies.
Phase B — ambiguous-case fallback. Needs an OpenAI-compatible client in orchestrator/, plus porting the tgi_compat shim (ADR-010) and function_calling structured output (config.py:49 — TGI 500s on json_schema). Real cost; that's why it's separate.
Phase C — Stage 5/6. Ledger state, headroom reservation before dispatch, switching cost, per-step attempt cap that escalates to a different agent.
One correction to the framing
On "Codex CLI and Claude Code act as agents/subagents/supervisors" — under maestro delegate, a delegated CLI is a worker, not a supervisor. It takes a prompt and returns output. The supervisor role stays in the orchestrator process. You can delegate a planning prompt and consume the plan, but if Claude Code spawns its own subagents internally, that nesting is opaque to the router — which matters for Stage 5, since usage inside that subtree can't be attributed per-step.

Nothing written or changed. Want me to build Phase A?

         super().__init__(f"LLM did not respond within {timeout:.0f}s.")
 
 
+class DeadlineExceededError(Exception):
+    """Raised by run_with_deadline when a call is abandoned. Never raised by a
+    provider, so catching it cannot swallow a real transport timeout."""
+    def __init__(self, name: str, timeout: float) -> None:
+        self.name = name
+        self.timeout = timeout
+        super().__init__(f"{name} exceeded its {timeout:.0f}s deadline.")
+
+
+def run_with_deadline(fn, *args, timeout: float, name: str, **kwargs):
+    """Run `fn` under a wall-clock deadline, abandoning it if it overruns.
+
+    A per-read socket timeout cannot bound this: a server that holds the
+    connection open while it generates never trips one, however long it thinks.
+
+    A daemon thread rather than a ThreadPoolExecutor, because the executor's
+    threads are non-daemon and concurrent.futures registers an atexit hook that
+    joins all of them. shutdown(wait=False) does not opt out of that -- an
+    abandoned call keeps the interpreter alive after the run is over, so the run
+    prints its summary and the process still never exits. Nothing in CPython can
+    cancel a thread blocked on a socket read, so the only question is whether it
+    outlives the process, and a daemon thread does not.
+
+    The context is copied so records logged from the thread keep the run's id.
+    """
+    outcome: dict[str, Any] = {}
+    context = _contextvars.copy_context()
+
+    def target() -> None:
+        try:
+            outcome["value"] = context.run(lambda: fn(*args, **kwargs))
+        except BaseException as exc:  # noqa: BLE001 -- re-raised in the caller
+            outcome["error"] = exc
+
+    thread = threading.Thread(target=target, name=name, daemon=True)
+    thread.start()
+    thread.join(timeout)
+
+    if thread.is_alive():
+        raise DeadlineExceededError(name, timeout)
+    if "error" in outcome:
+        raise outcome["error"]
+    return outcome["value"]
+
+

 def tool_call_limit(run_limit: int = TOOL_CALL_RUN_LIMIT) -> ToolCallLimitMiddleware:
-    """Bound one worker's tool calls per invocation, then end its run."""
-    return ToolCallLimitMiddleware(run_limit=run_limit, exit_behavior="end")
+    """Bound one worker's tool calls per invocation, leaving it a turn to report.
+
+    exit_behavior="continue" blocks further tool calls but keeps the agent loop
+    alive, so the worker still gets a turn to write up what it found. "end" stops
+    the run the moment the budget is gone: a worker that spent every turn
+    searching is cut off before it ever reports, and the supervisor receives a
+    bookkeeping stub instead of the findings. Blocked calls still count towards
+    the budget, so a model that keeps hammering a blocked tool runs out anyway.
+    """
+    return ToolCallLimitMiddleware(run_limit=run_limit, exit_behavior="continue")
+
+
+def single_call_limit(tool_name: str) -> ToolCallLimitMiddleware:
+    """Allow exactly one call to `tool_name` per invocation.
+
+    write_document and create_outline replace the whole file, so a second call
+    destroys what the first one wrote -- and the last call is reliably the
+    thinnest. Instructing the model not to do it does not hold; this makes it
+    impossible. Other tools are unaffected, so reading and editing still work.
+    """
+    return ToolCallLimitMiddleware(
+        tool_name=tool_name, run_limit=1, exit_behavior="continue"
+    )
+
+
+def run_worker(agent, name: str, state: State) -> str:
+    """Invoke a worker and always come back with a report.
+
+    create_agent calls the model directly, so llm/invoker.py's error handling
+    never sees a worker's turns: one bad generation aborts the entire graph.
+    A small model on a constrained decoder produces those regularly -- it can
+    degenerate mid-argument and the server rejects the request -- and that used
+    to discard the work of every team that had already finished. A supervisor
+    can route around a worker that failed; it cannot route around a traceback.
+
+    The deadline is wall-clock and covers the whole turn. The client's timeout
+    cannot stand in for it: httpx counts idle time between reads, so a server
+    that keeps the socket open while it generates never trips it, however long
+    it thinks.
+    """
+    logger.info("--> worker '%s' starting", name)
+    try:
+        result = run_with_deadline(
+            agent.invoke,
+            state,
+            timeout=WORKER_DEADLINE_SECONDS,
+            name=f"worker-{name}",
+        )
+    except DeadlineExceededError:
+        logger.error(
+            "Worker '%s' passed its %ds deadline; abandoning the turn",
+            name,
+            WORKER_DEADLINE_SECONDS,
+        )
+        return (
+            f"Worker '{name}' ran past its {WORKER_DEADLINE_SECONDS}s deadline and "
+            "produced no report. Anything it saved before then is still on disk."
+        )
+    except Exception as e:
+        logger.exception("Worker '%s' failed: %s", name, type(e).__name__)
+        detail = str(e)[:WORKER_ERROR_REPORT_CHARS]
+        return (
+            f"Worker '{name}' failed with {type(e).__name__} and produced no report. "
+            f"Anything it saved before failing is still on disk. Detail: {detail}"
+        )
+    report = agent_report(result)
+    logger.info("<-- worker '%s' reported: %s", name, report[:200])
+    return report
 
 
 def agent_report(result: dict) -> str:
     """Describe what a worker actually did.
 
     When the tool-call limit ends a run, the final message is a bookkeeping stub
-    ("Tool call limit reached: ..."). Handing that to the supervisor reads as
-    unfinished work and it routes straight back, so report the tool calls
-    instead -- that is the part the supervisor can act on.
+    ("Tool call limit reached: ..."), but the worker's real answer is usually a
+    turn or two further back. Returning the stub reads to the supervisor as
+    unfinished work and it routes straight back, so search backwards for the
+    last thing the worker actually said.
+
+    The tool calls are appended because prose is only a claim -- a worker saying
+    it wrote a file and a write_document call in the transcript are different
+    kinds of evidence, and the supervisor is asked to distinguish them.
     """
     messages = result["messages"]
-    text = (messages[-1].content or "").strip()
-    if text and not text.startswith("Tool call limit reached"):
-        return text
+
+    text = ""
+    for message in reversed(messages):
+        if getattr(message, "type", None) != "ai":
+            continue
+        candidate = (message.content or "").strip()
+        if candidate and not candidate.startswith("Tool call limit reached"):
+            text = candidate
+            break
 
     actions = []
     for message in messages:
         for call in getattr(message, "tool_calls", None) or []:
             target = call["args"].get("file_name", "")
             actions.append(f"{call['name']}({target})" if target else call["name"])
-    if not actions:
-        return "No action taken."
-    return f"Finished. Tools executed: {', '.join(actions)}."
+
+    if text:
+        return f"{text}\n\nTools executed: {', '.join(actions)}." if actions else text
+
+    # No prose anywhere: the worker spent every turn on tools and never wrote up
+    # what it found. The tool results are then the only findings that exist, and
+    # reporting bare tool names throws the whole invocation's work away -- the
+    # next team is left with nothing to write about but the request itself.
+    salvaged = _tool_results(messages)
+    if salvaged:
+        return f"No written report -- raw tool results follow.\n\n{salvaged}"
+    if actions:
+        return f"Finished. Tools executed: {', '.join(actions)}."
+    return "No action taken."
+
+
+def _tool_results(messages: list) -> str:
+    """Tool output that carries findings, trimmed to keep a supervisor readable.
+
+    Skips the artificial error results the tool-call limit injects; those report
+    the budget, not the world.
+    """
+    parts, seen = [], set()
+    for message in messages:
+        if getattr(message, "type", None) != "tool":
+            continue
+        if getattr(message, "status", None) == "error":
+            continue
+        content = (message.content or "").strip()
+        if not content or content in seen:
+            continue
+        seen.add(content)
+        if len(content) > TOOL_RESULT_SALVAGE_CHARS:
+            content = content[:TOOL_RESULT_SALVAGE_CHARS] + " ...[truncated]"
+        parts.append(f"[{getattr(message, 'name', None) or 'tool'}] {content}")
+    return "\n\n".join(parts)

+def team_report(result: dict, sent: int) -> str:
+    """Everything a team produced this turn, not just its closing message.
+
+    A team's last message is whichever worker happened to report last, which
+    after a forced FINISH is often the thinnest one. Collapsing to it throws
+    away findings the team really did produce -- and those findings are the only
+    thing the next team has to work from.
+    """
+    parts, seen = [], set()
+    for message in result["messages"][sent:]:
+        text = (message.content or "").strip()
+        if not text or text in seen:
+            continue
+        seen.add(text)
+        name = getattr(message, "name", None)
+        parts.append(f"[{name}] {text}" if name else text)
+    return "\n\n".join(parts) or "No output produced."
+
+
+def _call_team(graph, name: str, state: State) -> Command[Literal["supervisor"]]:
+    """Run a team over the whole conversation and fold back everything it added.
+
+    The full history goes in, not just the last message: a team invoked a second
+    time would otherwise be handed its own previous report and research itself,
+    and the writing team would never see the original request -- including the
+    file name it is supposed to produce.
+    """
+    logger.info(">>> %s starting (recursion_limit=%d)", name, TEAM_RECURSION_LIMIT)
+    sent = state["messages"]
+    result = graph.invoke({"messages": sent}, TEAM_CONFIG)
+    report = team_report(result, len(sent))
+    logger.info(
+        "<<< %s finished after %d new message(s)", name, len(result["messages"]) - len(sent)
+    )
     return Command(
-        update={
-            "messages": [
-                HumanMessage(
-                    content=result["messages"][-1].content, name="research_team"
-                )
-            ]
-        },
+        update={"messages": [HumanMessage(content=report, name=name)]},
         goto="supervisor",
     )
 
 
+def call_research_team(state: State) -> Command[Literal["supervisor"]]:
+    return _call_team(research_graph, "research_team", state)
+
